## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for items from one half (GOOD half), increases by 1 for items from the other half (BAD half)
- We don't know which color (BLUE or GREEN) is good yet - need to explore both
- Robots start in center, need to navigate to source (BLACK floor), pick up items, return to nest (WHITE floor)
- Key perceptions: floor color (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=middle), wall colors (RED=nest side, BLUE/GREEN=source halves)

**Strategy:**
- A robot should: explore → go to source → pick up item → return to nest
- To minimize score: deliver items from the GOOD half (decreases score)
- Need to target specific color: either BLUE or GREEN walls

**State design ideas:**

1. **Simple 2-state**: Explore until BLACK floor, then go to WHITE (GoToColor with white/red)
2. **3-state foraging**: Explore → GoToColor(BLACK source) → GoToColor(RED/WHITE nest)
3. **Targeted foraging**: Go to specific colored wall (BLUE or GREEN), then return to nest

**Color codes**: {1,2,3,4,5,6} - need to figure out mapping. Based on typical ARGoS: 1=black, 2=gray, 3=white, 4=red(?), 5=blue(?), 6=green(?) or similar.

Actually the colors for `--clr` and `--l` conditions: typically in AutoMoDe: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=magenta, 5=yellow, 6=cyan (for LED colors). But for floor/objects it may differ.

Given the wall colors mentioned: RED wall (nest side), BLUE and GREEN walls (source sides). The `--clr` parameter targets object colors seen by camera.

Let me design diverse machines covering different strategies:

1. **Elitism**: No previous best - design fresh
2. **Strategy A**: Go to BLUE walls, then go to RED wall (nest)
3. **Strategy B**: Go to GREEN walls, then go to RED wall (nest)
4. **Strategy C**: Explore on BLACK, go to WHITE on WHITE floor
5. **Strategy D**: 3-state with intermediate exploration

Let me assume color IDs: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan for camera. For floor: BLACK=C0, GRAY=C1, WHITE=C2.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Since I don't have explicit scores from round 2, I need to reason about what strategies are most likely to work based on the mission structure.

**Key insights:**
- BLACK floor = source area (robots get items there)
- WHITE floor = nest area (items delivered there)
- GRAY floor = transit zone
- RED wall = nest side wall
- BLUE/GREEN walls = source side walls (one type scores -1, other +1)
- We need to deliver items from the GOOD half (unknown which color yet)

**Core foraging loop:**
1. Navigate to source (BLACK floor)
2. Pick specific item type (go toward BLUE or GREEN wall)
3. Return to nest (WHITE floor / RED wall)

**What likely works best:**
- GoToColor targeting RED wall (clr=1) to find nest
- GoToColor targeting BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) to find specific source half
- Floor color transitions: BLACK→carrying item, WHITE→at nest

**State machine logic:**
- State A: Navigate toward source color (BLUE or GREEN wall) until on BLACK floor
- State B: Navigate toward RED wall (nest) until on WHITE floor
- Transition: BLACK floor → go to nest; WHITE floor → go to source

**Color mapping assumption** (standard AutoMoDe): 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

Let me design 14 machines with diversity between BLUE-targeting and GREEN-targeting strategies, plus various topologies:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 3 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to think more carefully about what makes a good foraging FSM for this mission.

**Key observations:**
1. The mission requires delivering items from the GOOD half (decreases score). We need to figure out if BLUE or GREEN is good.
2. The basic foraging loop: go to source → get item → return to nest → repeat
3. Floor colors are the most reliable signal: BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=transit

**Problems with my previous designs:**
- GoToColor with clr=3 (blue) or clr=2 (green) targets wall blocks, but robots need to navigate toward those walls
- The transition condition "on BLACK floor" is good for detecting arrival at source
- "on WHITE floor" detects arrival at nest
- Going toward RED wall (clr=1) should reliably navigate to nest

**Better strategy:**
- State 0: GoToColor(BLUE wall, clr=3) → move toward blue source. Transition: BLACK floor → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(RED wall, clr=1) → move toward nest. Transition: WHITE floor → State 0

OR symmetrically with GREEN.

**Alternative using floor colors more directly:**
- On WHITE floor (nest): go toward BLACK (explore/source)
- On BLACK floor (source): go toward WHITE/RED (nest)
- On GRAY: continue in same direction

**The "GoToColor targeting floor" vs "GoToColor targeting wall":**
- GoToColor targets objects seen by camera, not floor color
- Floor colors are detected by floor sensor → used in transition conditions (C=0,1,2)
- Wall colors are seen by camera → used in GoToColor behavior and C=7 condition

**Refined design - 3 state targeted foraging:**
- S0: Explore (random walk) until see BLUE or BLACK floor
- S1: GoToColor(BLUE=3) until BLACK floor → go to S2
- S2: GoToColor(RED=1) until WHITE floor → go to S0/S1

Let me also try variations with different transition probabilities and mixing BLUE vs GREEN targeting.

**Important realization:** Since we're trying BOTH blue and green strategies, whichever one scores lower will be selected by evolution. Let me include both variants.

**Also trying:** Using GoAwayColor(RED) to move away from nest = move toward source.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 4 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to reconsider my approach fundamentally.

**Critical reflection on my designs:**

1. My 2-state machines (GoToColor→GoToColor) might work but need refinement
2. The key question: which color (BLUE=clr3 or GREEN=clr2) decreases the score?
3. I've been trying both but without feedback I can't know which is better

**Fundamental issue I may have missed:**
- When a robot enters a source half, it gets an item automatically
- The robot needs to then return to the WHITE nest area
- The floor signal (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest) is the key trigger

**Better transition logic:**
- At source (BLACK floor): STOP collecting, go to nest. Trigger: BLACK floor detected
- At nest (WHITE floor): STOP delivering, go to source. Trigger: WHITE floor detected
- The GoToColor behavior should point toward the colored wall to target the correct half

**Probability tuning:**
- High probability (0.9-0.95) for floor-based transitions = fast response
- The `--p` for floor conditions is a probability of firing when condition is true

**New insight - simpler might be better:**
- 2-state: S0=GoToColor(BLUE wall) → on BLACK floor → S1=GoToColor(RED wall) → on WHITE floor → S0
- This is the core foraging loop, very clean

**Exploration additions:**
- Try GoAwayColor(RED) as "go toward source" behavior
- Try Exploration with high turning rate as alternative to GoToColor
- Try 4-state with dedicated "carry" and "empty" states

**Diversity strategy for round 5:**
- 4 machines targeting BLUE (clr=3) with different topologies
- 4 machines targeting GREEN (clr=2) with different topologies  
- 3 machines with mixed/exploration strategies
- 3 machines with 4 states for richer behavior

Let me write clean, correct FSMs:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 5 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to fundamentally reconsider my approach. The lack of feedback means I can't know which designs scored best, so I should think harder about the problem structure.

**Critical analysis of the mission:**

1. Score is MINIMISED. Items from one half give -1 (good), the other +1 (bad).
2. We don't know which half (BLUE=clr3 or GREEN=clr2) is good.
3. The optimal strategy: always target the GOOD half and deliver quickly.

**What could go wrong with my designs:**
- If I'm targeting the WRONG color (e.g., GREEN when BLUE is good), all my GREEN-targeting machines score positively (bad!)
- The evolutionary optimizer should discover which is better, but I need to give it enough budget on both

**Key realization about GoToColor:**
- GoToColor(clr=3) means go toward BLUE objects in camera view
- GoToColor(clr=2) means go toward GREEN objects
- GoToColor(clr=1) means go toward RED objects (nest wall)
- These are wall colors, which persist in view as robot approaches

**Realization about floor-based transitions:**
- C=0: BLACK floor → at source (just picked up item)
- C=2: WHITE floor → at nest (just delivered item)
- These are the RIGHT triggers for the foraging cycle

**New strategies to try:**
1. **Purely floor-driven**: No camera targeting, just explore until floor changes
   - S0: Explore → on BLACK → S1; S1: GoToColor(RED) → on WHITE → S0
2. **Mixed signal**: Use camera to find source half but floor to detect arrival
3. **Avoid wrong color**: GoAwayColor from one source half while going to the other

**Important insight I've been missing:**
- Maybe I should try GoToColor(clr=1) for BOTH states - once at source, go to RED; once at nest, go away from RED
- GoAwayColor(RED) would push robots toward source area

**New diverse set:**

Let me try completely fresh topologies:
- GoAwayColor(RED) to reach source + GoToColor(RED) to reach nest
- Pure exploration with floor triggers  
- 4-state with carrier/empty distinction

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 6 rounds with no explicit score feedback, I need to take stock and think differently.

**What I know for certain:**
- Score is minimized; delivering GOOD items (one specific color) decreases score by 1
- BLUE walls (clr=3) or GREEN walls (clr=2) mark the two source halves
- RED wall (clr=1) marks the nest side
- Floor: BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=transit

**What I've been doing wrong potentially:**
- I've been submitting very similar machines every round with minor variations
- Without score feedback, I can't tell which color is good
- My designs may be stuck in the same local optima

**Fresh thinking:**

The core issue: I need robots to:
1. Find the source (BLACK floor)
2. Specifically collect from the GOOD half (either BLUE or GREEN side)
3. Return to nest (WHITE floor)

The problem is that GoToColor targets the NEAREST colored object. If robots are at the source, they should go TOWARD the good-colored wall. But they need to be at the source first.

**Key insight I may have missed:** 
- When transitioning from S0→S1 on BLACK floor, the robot is ALREADY at the source and has an item
- So S1 just needs to navigate back to nest - GoToColor(RED, clr=1) makes sense
- The question is whether targeting BLUE or GREEN in S0 matters for which HALF they end up in

**Alternative approach - use WHITE floor as "at nest" trigger:**
- At nest (WHITE), go toward source: GoAwayColor(RED) pushes away from nest wall
- At source (BLACK), go toward nest: GoToColor(RED)

**Another approach - use neighbor signals:**
- Robots with items could signal with LEDs
- Other robots could follow LED carriers

**Most important: try radically different structures**

Let me try:
1. GoAwayColor(RED) to reach source + floor trigger to return
2. Pure exploration cycles
3. Attraction-based foraging (follow others)
4. Very high probability transitions for speed

**Corrected understanding of destination indexing:**
With 3 states (0,1,2), leaving state 0: others are [1,2], so D=0→state1, D=1→state2
With 3 states, leaving state 1: others are [0,2], so D=0→state0, D=1→state2
With 3 states, leaving state 2: others are [0,1], so D=0→state0, D=1→state1

Let me be very careful with this in my FSMs.

# ROUND 8/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 7 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to fundamentally reconsider my strategy. I've been submitting very similar machines repeatedly. Let me think more carefully.

**Deep analysis of the problem:**

The foraging loop requires:
1. Robot starts at center (GRAY floor)
2. Navigate to source (BLACK floor) - specifically the GOOD half
3. Automatically picks up item when entering source
4. Navigate back to nest (WHITE floor)
5. Item delivered automatically
6. Repeat

**Critical issues with my previous designs:**

1. **GoToColor targeting**: When I use GoToColor(BLUE=clr3), the robot goes toward the nearest BLUE object. But BLUE walls are at the FAR end of the arena. The robot should be able to see them from a distance and navigate toward them.

2. **The "on BLACK floor" transition**: When the robot is on BLACK floor, it's AT the source and has an item. It should then return to nest. This transition makes sense.

3. **The "on WHITE floor" transition**: When the robot is on WHITE floor, it's AT the nest and has delivered. It should go back to source. This transition makes sense.

4. **What might be wrong**: Maybe my transitions fire too eagerly (high probability 0.9) causing robots to immediately leave the source or nest before completing their task. But actually, with floor sensors, as soon as they step on BLACK, they fire.

**New ideas:**

1. **Try lower transition probabilities** for floor conditions - maybe 0.5 or 0.7 so robots spend more time in each zone
2. **Use C=5 (random probability)** as a timeout mechanism
3. **Try attraction behavior** in nest to cluster robots at delivery point
4. **Try different color combinations** - what if I try clr=4,5,6?

Wait - let me reconsider the color mapping. The format says `--clr` is in {1,2,3,4,5,6} and `--l` (for C=7) is in {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The arena has RED, BLUE, GREEN walls. Standard AutoMoDe color mapping is typically:
- 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

So RED=1, GREEN=2, BLUE=3. That's what I've been using.

**But wait** - what if the "good" color is actually determined randomly per run? The mission says "an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1" - this might be fixed (always BLUE good or always GREEN good), or it might vary.

**Key strategic decision**: I should commit more strongly to ONE color being good and optimize that, rather than splitting effort. But since I don't know which, let me try 7 BLUE-targeting + 6 GREEN-targeting, or try to use BOTH in single machines.

**New topology idea - use BOTH source halves but prefer one:**
- S0: GoToColor(BLUE) until BLACK floor → S1
- S1: GoToColor(RED/nest) until WHITE floor → S0
- This targets BLUE specifically

**Alternative - exploit LED colors for communication:**
- Robots carrying items could set LED to one color
- Others could follow robots with items (attraction to LED color via C=7)

**Most promising new idea**: What if I use a 4-state machine where:
- S0: GoToColor(BLUE) - heading to source
- S1: GoToColor(RED) - returning to nest (triggered by BLACK floor)
- S2: Stop briefly at nest (triggered by WHITE floor)  
- S3: Exploration if lost (triggered by random probability)

Let me also try **much higher** transition probabilities (0.99) to make transitions near-deterministic.

And let me try a machine where the robot goes to WHITE floor first (toward nest from start), then bounces to source.

**13 machines:**

# ROUND 9/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 8 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to be more systematic and try genuinely different approaches. Let me think about what I might be fundamentally missing.

**Core problem analysis:**

The foraging loop: source → nest → source → nest...

**What behaviors are available and what do they do physically:**
- B=8 GoToColor: moves toward nearest object of given color (wall blocks)
- B=9 GoAwayColor: moves away from nearest object of given color
- B=0 Exploration: random walk
- B=4 Attraction: moves toward nearby robots
- B=5 Repulsion: moves away from nearby robots

**Transition conditions:**
- C=0: BLACK floor (at source)
- C=2: WHITE floor (at nest)  
- C=7: sees a given color with camera

**What I think happens with my designs:**
- GoToColor(BLUE=3): robot steers toward blue wall blocks at source
- Once it reaches BLACK floor, it has an item
- GoToColor(RED=1): robot steers toward red wall at nest side
- Once it reaches WHITE floor, item delivered

This should work! But maybe the issue is that:
1. Robots start on GRAY floor, not near the source or nest
2. GoToColor(BLUE) from center should work if robots can see blue walls
3. GoToColor(RED) from source should work to find nest

**Perhaps the issue is that I don't know which color is "good"** and I've been wasting half my budget on the wrong color.

**New strategy: Try to use BOTH colors in same machine:**
- A robot that visits BOTH source halves but delivers only when coming from one specific side
- OR: a robot that explores randomly but uses floor colors to navigate home

**Radical new idea: Floor-only navigation:**
- S0: Exploration (random walk) until BLACK floor detected → S1
- S1: GoToColor(RED) until WHITE floor detected → S0
- This way, robots collect from WHATEVER half they happen to reach, which is 50/50 good/bad

But 50/50 would score ~0 on average. We need to be selective.

**Better idea: Target only one color, accept that it might be wrong:**
- If BLUE is good: GoToColor(BLUE) → BLACK → GoToColor(RED) → WHITE scores -N
- If BLUE is bad: GoToColor(BLUE) → BLACK → GoToColor(RED) → WHITE scores +N

The evolutionary optimizer over 600 sims per round SHOULD be able to figure out which is better. But I'm not getting feedback on which performs better!

**Wait - I realize I've been confused about the conversation structure.** The optimizer runs 600 simulations per round testing my 14 machines. The BEST performing machine should emerge. But I'm not told what scored best!

**New approach: Try machines that are clearly better regardless of which color is good:**

One approach: make robots explore randomly (no color preference) but return home efficiently. This gives ~0 score (50% good items, 50% bad).

Another approach: Use LED colors to distinguish carriers. Set LED to specific color when heading to source, different when returning. Use C=7 (perceive color) to follow carriers.

**Most promising new direction: Stigmergy via LEDs**
- S0 (going to source): LED=yellow, GoToColor(BLUE or GREEN)
- S1 (returning to nest): LED=magenta, GoToColor(RED)  
- Other robots: if they see magenta (carrier going to nest), they know the path
- But this doesn't help SELECT which color is good

**Actually the simplest fix**: Use GoAwayColor(RED) to go toward source (away from red wall = toward source end). Then any item picked up is random 50/50. Score ~0. Not great.

**The REAL solution**: Pick one color confidently and optimize the navigation:
- Fast, efficient navigation to BLUE half specifically
- Fast return to nest
- Minimize time wasted

Let me try machines with:
1. Very direct navigation (GoToColor with no exploration states)
2. Fast floor-triggered transitions
3. One color per machine (BLUE or GREEN)
4. Varying topologies for robustness

**Also try**: What if I use GoToColor(BLACK=?) - wait, there's no BLACK color for GoToColor, only wall colors.

**New topology to try:**
- 3-state: S0=GoToColor(BLUE), S1=GoToColor(RED), S2=GoToColor(BLUE)
  - S0→S1 on BLACK (got item, go home)
  - S1→S2 on WHITE (delivered, go back)  
  - S2→S1 on BLACK (same as S0 but different state for tracking)
  
This is essentially the same as 2-state but with explicit carrier/empty distinction.

**Try: Random exploration for finding source + directed return:**
- S0: Exploration until BLACK floor → S1
- S1: GoToColor(RED) until WHITE floor → S0

This is unbiased but might score 0 on average.

**Try: GoToColor with very specific color targeting:**
- What if one source half wall color is actually color 4, 5, or 6?
- I've been assuming BLUE=3, GREEN=2 based on standard AutoMoDe mapping
- Let me try clr=4,5,6 as well

Actually the problem statement says "BLUE" and "GREEN" walls. Standard AutoMoDe: 1=red,2=green,3=blue. This is consistent with what I've been using.

**Final strategy for round 9:**
- 4 machines: clean 2-state BLUE-targeting (variations in probability)
- 4 machines: clean 2-state GREEN-targeting (variations in probability)  
- 2 machines: 3-state BLUE with exploration recovery
- 2 machines: 3-state GREEN with exploration recovery
- 1 machine: pure floor-based (exploration + GoToColor RED)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. I've been running 9 rounds without explicit score feedback, so I need to make my best judgment about what works.

**Final analysis:**

The core foraging loop I've been using (GoToColor(source color) → BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED nest) → WHITE floor → repeat) is logically sound. The key unknowns are:
1. Which color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) is the "good" one
2. Whether my navigation is efficient enough

**What I'm most confident about:**
- The 2-state machine `GoToColor(X) → [BLACK→] GoToColor(RED) → [WHITE→]` is the cleanest foraging loop
- HIGH transition probabilities (0.99) are better than lower ones for responsive behavior
- Both BLUE and GREEN targeting need to be represented

**What I should try differently in final round:**
- More aggressive exploration of the state space
- Include machines that target BOTH colors (via multiple transitions or states)
- Try machines where robots use C=1 (GRAY floor) as a trigger to START navigating to source
- Try machines with attraction behavior to create efficient trails

**Key insight for final round:**
Since I've been splitting evenly between BLUE and GREEN, if one is consistently better the optimizer should have found it. Let me try a different ratio: 7 BLUE + 5 GREEN, or try machines that can handle both.

**Novel idea: Sequential color targeting**
- S0: GoToColor(BLUE) until BLACK → S1, also if at WHITE (just delivered) → S0
- S1: GoToColor(RED) until WHITE → S0
This creates a tight loop specifically for BLUE items.

**Another novel idea: Use GRAY floor detection**
- When on GRAY floor moving toward source: GoToColor(BLUE)
- When on BLACK floor (just got item): GoToColor(RED)
- When on WHITE floor (just delivered): switch back

**Final 12 machines - balanced, high-quality designs:**

I'll go with:
- 3 clean 2-state BLUE machines (varying probabilities: 0.99, 0.95, 0.9)
- 3 clean 2-state GREEN machines (varying probabilities: 0.99, 0.95, 0.9)
- 2 3-state BLUE with recovery
- 2 3-state GREEN with recovery
- 1 GoAwayRED + GoToRED (floor-triggered, color-agnostic)
- 1 4-state BLUE with full cycle

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
